106\\t└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘\\n   107\\t\\n   108\\t```\\n   109\\t\\n   110\\t## 3. Component Breakdown\\n   111\\t\\n   112\\t### 3.1. Electron Renderer Process Components\\n   113\\t\\n   114\\t-   **`AgentChatPane.tsx`**: The primary UI for chat. It uses the `useAgentChat` hook.\\n   115\\t-   **`useAgentChat.ts` Hook**:\\n   116\\t    -   Manages chat state (messages, loading, errors).\\n   117\\t    -   Interacts with `ChatOrchestratorService` to send messages and receive streamed responses.\\n   118\\t    -   When \\\"Claude Code (CLI)\\\" is selected as the provider, `ChatOrchestratorService` will route requests to `ClaudeCodeCliAgentLanguageModelLive`.\\n   119\\t-   **`ClaudeCodeCliAgentLanguageModelLive.ts` (`src/services/ai/providers/claude_code_cli/`)**:\\n   120\\t    -   Implements the `AgentLanguageModel.Tag` interface.\\n   121\\t    -   Converts `AgentLanguageModel` method calls (e.g., `streamText`) into IPC requests to the Electron main process using `window.electronAPI.claudeCode.streamChat()` (exposed via preload script).\\n   122\\t    -   Adapts parameters to the format expected by the IPC bridge (ultimately for the Claude CLI).\\n   123\\t    -   Receives streamed responses (or errors) from IPC and maps them to the `AiResponse` type (or `AiProviderError`).\\n   124\\t-   **IPC Context Exposer (`src/helpers/ipc/claude_code/claude-code-context.ts` & `src/helpers/ipc/context-exposer.ts`):**\\n   125\\t    -   The preload script (`src/preload.ts`) calls `exposeContexts()`.\\n   126\\t    -   `exposeContexts()` calls specific exposers like `exposeClaudeCodeContext()`.\\n   127\\t    -   `exposeClaudeCodeContext()` uses `contextBridge.exposeInMainWorld(\\\"electronAPI.claudeCode\\\", ...)` to provide renderer-safe functions for invoking Claude Code related IPC channels.\\n   128\\t    -   These functions handle the low-level `ipcRenderer.invoke` or `ipcRenderer.send/on` logic for communication with the main process.\\n   129\\t\\n   130\\t### 3.2. Electron Main Process Components\\n   131\\t\\n   132\\t-   **Claude Code IPC Listeners (`src/main-claude-websocket.ts` -> `setupClaudeWebSocketHandler()` called from `src/main.ts`):**\\n   133\\t    -   **Non-streaming (`claude-code:chat-completion` channel):**\\n   134\\t        -   Receives requests from the renderer via `ipcMain.handle`.\\n   135\\t        -   Directly spawns the `@anthropic-ai/claude-code` CLI using `child_process.spawn` for simple, non-interactive commands. This handler is a **fallback/alternative** to the WebSocket bridge for potentially simpler, non-streaming requests.\\n   136\\t        -   _Note: The primary streaming mechanism now uses the WebSocket bridge._\\n   137\\t    -   **Streaming (`claude-code:chat-stream` channel):**\\n   138\\t        -   Receives requests from the renderer via `ipcMain.on`.\\n   139\\t        -   Establishes a WebSocket connection to the external `Claude Bridge Service` (running on `ws://localhost:45671`).\\n   140\\t        -   Sends a JSON message to the bridge service containing the `requestId` and CLI arguments.\\n   141\\t        -   Listens for `claude_stream_chunk`, `claude_stream_done`, `claude_stream_error` messages from the bridge service over WebSocket.\\n   142\\t        -   Relays these messages back to the originating renderer window using `event.sender.send()`.\\n   143\\t        -   Handles stream cancellation requests from the renderer by closing the WebSocket connection or sending a cancel message to the bridge.\\n   144\\t-   **Database Proxying Logic (in `main-claude-websocket.ts`):**\\n   145\\t    -   The main process handlers for saving sessions and messages (`saveSessionToDatabase`, `saveMessageToDatabase`, etc.) also use the WebSocket bridge to delegate these operations to the `Claude Bridge Service`, which has direct PGlite access.\\n   146\\t-   **IPC Channel Definitions (`src/helpers/ipc/claude_code/claude-code-channels.ts`):**\\n   147\\t    -   Defines constants for channel names (e.g., `CLAUDE_CODE_CHAT_STREAM_CHANNEL`).\\n   148\\t\\n   149\\t### 3.3. Claude Bridge Service (External Node.js Process)\\n   150\\t-   **File:** `src/services/claude-bridge-service.js`\\n   151\\t-   **Purpose:** A standalone Node.js script that acts as a bridge between the Electron main process and the Claude Code CLI, and also directly manages database operations.\\n   152\\t-   **Key Components:**\\n   153\\t    -   **WebSocket Server:**\\n   154\\t        -   Listens on `ws://localhost:45671`.\\n   155\\t        -   Handles incoming messages from the Electron main process.\\n   156\\t        -   Differentiates between Claude CLI command requests and database operation requests based on message `type`.\\n   157\\t    -   **Claude CLI Execution (`node-pty`):**\\n   158\\t        -   Receives CLI arguments from Electron.\\n   159\\t        -   Uses `node-pty` to spawn the `@anthropic-ai/claude-code` CLI as a pseudo-terminal, which can be more robust for interactive CLIs than `child_process`.\\n   160\\t        -   Forces `--output-format stream-json` for streaming.\\n   161\\t        -   Captures `stdout` from the CLI.\\n   162\\t        -   Parses the line-separated JSON objects from `stream-json` output.\\n   163\\t        -   Sends each parsed JSON object (chunk) back to the Electron main process over WebSocket.\\n   164\\t        -   Handles CLI exit and error events, relaying them back.\\n   165\\t    -   **PGlite Database Client:**\\n   166\\t        -   Initializes a `PGlite` client instance using the **synchronized database path** (same as Electron's main process).\\n   167\\t        -   Executes SQL queries received from Electron (via WebSocket) for saving sessions, messages, and tool executions.\\n   168\\t        -   Handles schema creation and basic migrations (e.g., adding missing columns).\\n   169\\t    -   **Path Management:**\\n   170\\t        -   Calculates the database directory path in a platform-aware manner to match `app.getPath(\\\"userData\\\")` used by Electron, ensuring both processes access the same database file. (Fix `024-database-path-synchronization.md`)\\n   171\\t    -   **Logging:** Writes logs to `claude-bridge-service.log` in the user's home directory.\\n   172\\t\\n   173\\t### 3.4. `@anthropic-ai/claude-code` CLI (External Subprocess)\\n   174\\t-   The actual command-line tool provided by Anthropic.\\n   175\\t-   Spawned by the `Claude Bridge Service` using `node-pty`.\\n   176\\t-   Interacts with Anthropic's backend APIs.\\n   177\\t-   Requires `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` environment variable (passed by the bridge service).\\n   178\\t-   Its `stdout` (especially in `stream-json` mode) is the source of data for streaming back to the application.\\n   179\\t\\n   180\\t## 4. Communication Protocols & Data Flow\\n   181\\t\\n   182\\t### 4.1. Renderer to Main Process (Electron IPC)\\n   183\\t-   **Channel:** `claude-code:chat-stream` (for streaming), `claude-code:chat-completion` (for non-streaming).\\n   184\\t-   **Payload (`ClaudeExecParams` type in `src/types.d.ts`):**\\n   185\\t    ```typescript\\n   186\\t    interface ClaudeExecParams {\\n   187\\t      messages: Array<{role: string; content: string}>;\\n   188\\t      model?: string;\\n   189\\t      max_tokens?: number;\\n   190\\t      temperature?: number;\\n   191\\t      sessionId?: string; // For DB persistence\\n   192\\t      // ... other CLI compatible params ...\\n   193\\t    }\\n   194\\t    ```\\n   195\\t-   **Streaming Flow:**\\n   196\\t    1.  Renderer calls `window.electronAPI.claudeCode.streamChat(params, onChunk, onDone, onError)`.\\n   197\\t    2.  Preload script sends message to main process on `claude-code:chat-stream` with `requestId` and `params`.\\n   198\\t    3.  Main process listens for `${channel}:chunk`, `${channel}:done`, `${channel}:error` events from renderer, tagged with `requestId`.\\n   199\\t\\n   200\\t### 4.2. Main Process to Bridge Service (WebSocket)\\n   201\\t-   **URL:** `ws://localhost:45671`\\n   202\\t-   **Message Format (Main -> Bridge for CLI command):**\\n   203\\t    ```json\\n   204\\t    {\\n   205\\t      \\\"id\\\": \\\"unique-request-id-from-ipc\\\", // Passed through from renderer\\n   206\\t      \\\"args\\\": [\\\"-p\\\", \\\"prompt text\\\", \\\"--output-format\\\", \\\"stream-json\\\", ...] // Array of CLI arguments\\n   207\\t    }\\n   208\\t    ```\\n   209\\t-   **Message Format (Main -> Bridge for DB operation):**\\n   210\\t    ```json\\n   211\\t    {\\n   212\\t      \\\"type\\\": \\\"db\\\",\\n   213\\t      \\\"id\\\": \\\"db-request-id\\\",\\n   214\\t      \\\"operation\\\": \\\"saveMessage\\\", // e.g., \\\"saveSession\\\", \\\"getMessagesForSession\\\"\\n   215\\t      \\\"params\\\": { /* operation-specific parameters */ }\\n   216\\t    }\\n   217\\t    ```\\n   218\\t-   **Message Format (Bridge -> Main for CLI Stream Chunks):**\\n   219\\t    ```json\\n   220\\t    {\\n   221\\t      \\\"id\\\": \\\"unique-request-id-from-ipc\\\",\\n   222\\t      \\\"type\\\": \\\"claude_stream_chunk\\\",\\n   223\\t      \\\"payload\\\": { /* Parsed JSON object from Claude CLI's stream-json output */ }\\n   224\\t    }\\n   225\\t    ```\\n   226\\t-   **Message Format (Bridge -> Main for CLI Stream Done/Error):**\\n   227\\t    ```json\\n   228\\t    { \\\"id\\\": \\\"unique-request-id-from-ipc\\\", \\\"type\\\": \\\"claude_stream_done\\\", \\\"exitCode\\\": 0 }\\n   229\\t    { \\\"id\\\": \\\"unique-request-id-from-ipc\\\", \\\"type\\\": \\\"claude_stream_error\\\", \\\"error\\\": \\\"Error message\\\" }\\n   230\\t    ```\\n   231\\t-   **Message Format (Bridge -> Main for DB Result/Error):**\\n   232\\t    ```json\\n   233\\t    { \\\"id\\\": \\\"db-request-id\\\", \\\"type\\\": \\\"db_result\\\", \\\"result\\\": { /* query result */ } }\\n   234\\t    { \\\"id\\\": \\\"db-request-id\\\", \\\"type\\\": \\\"db_error\\\", \\\"error\\\": \\\"Error message\\\" }\\n   235\\t    ```\\n   236\\t\\n   237\\t### 4.3. Bridge Service to Claude CLI (node-pty)\\n   238\\t-   Standard `stdin`, `stdout`, `stderr` interaction with the spawned CLI process.\\n   239\\t-   Bridge service passes arguments and environment variables (like `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`) when spawning.\\n   240\\t-   Reads `stream-json` output from CLI's `stdout`.\\n   241\\t\\n   242\\t## 5. Key Functionalities\\n   243\\t\\n   244\\t### 5.1. Chat Completions (Streaming & Non-Streaming)\\n   245\\t-   The `ClaudeCodeCliAgentLanguageModelLive` implements `streamText` and `generateText`.\\n   246\\t-   **Streaming:**\\n   247\\t    -   Uses the `claude-code:chat-stream` IPC channel.\\n   248\\t    -   Bridge service uses `node-pty` and parses `stream-json` output.\\n   249\\t    -   Chunks are relayed back to the renderer and progressively update the UI.\\n   250\\t-   **Non-Streaming (Fallback):**\\n   251\\t    -   Uses the `claude-code:chat-completion` IPC channel.\\n   252\\t    -   Main process handler uses `child_process.spawn` (simpler for single response).\\n   253\\t    -   Entire response is returned as a single payload.\\n   254\\t\\n   255\\t### 5.2. Tool Use / Function Calling\\n   256\\t-   Claude Code CLI supports tools.\\n   257\\t-   The system is designed to handle this:\\n   258\\t    1.  `ClaudeCodeCliAgentLanguageModelLive` formats tools from `AiToolkit` into the CLI's expected format and includes them in the request.\\n   259\\t    2.  The CLI, if it decides to use a tool, will output a `tool_use` content block (as per Anthropic's format).\\n   260\\t    3.  The bridge service parses this and sends it as part of a `claude_stream_chunk` payload.\\n   261\\t    4.  The Electron main process relays this tool call information.\\n   262\\t    5.  `ChatOrchestratorService` in the renderer receives the `AiResponse` containing `toolCalls`.\\n   263\\t    6.  It invokes `ToolHandlerService.executeTool()`.\\n   264\\t    7.  The tool's result (stringified JSON) is sent back to the Claude Code CLI via another call through the same IPC/WebSocket/PTY chain, as a `role: \\\"tool\\\"` message.\\n   265\\t\\n   266\\t### 5.3. Message and Session Persistence (via Bridge Service)\\n   267\\t-   The Electron main process (`main-claude-websocket.ts`) is responsible for initiating database saves.\\n   268\\t-   After receiving messages or completing interactions with the Claude CLI (via the bridge), it sends specific database operation requests (e.g., `saveSession`, `saveMessage`, `saveToolCall`) to the `Claude Bridge Service` over WebSocket.\\n   269\\t-   The bridge service directly executes these PGlite operations using its own PGlite client instance that points to the shared database file.\\n   270\\t-   This keeps direct database access outside of Electron's renderer and main process for this specific provider, centralizing it in the bridge.\\n   271\\t\\n   272\\t## 6. Configuration Management\\n   273\\t-   **`ConfigurationService` (`src/services/configuration/`):**\\n   274\\t    -   Stores `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` (as a secret).\\n   275\\t    -   Stores `CLAUDE_CODE_CLI_PATH` (optional, if CLI is not in system `PATH`).\\n   276\\t    -   Stores `CLAUDE_CODE_PROVIDER_ENABLED` and `CLAUDE_CODE_DEFAULT_MODEL`.\\n   277\\t-   **Bridge Service (`claude-bridge-service.js`):**\\n   278\\t    -   Reads `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` from its own environment variables (which Electron main process should ensure are set when spawning the bridge, or the bridge reads from system env).\\n   279\\t    -   Hardcoded WebSocket port (`45671`).\\n   280\\t    -   Computes DB path dynamically.\\n   281\\t\\n   282\\t## 7. Error Handling\\n   283\\t-   **CLI Errors:** Bridge service captures `stderr` and non-zero exit codes from `node-pty`, sending them as `claude_stream_error` messages over WebSocket.\\n   284\\t-   **WebSocket Errors:** Electron main process handles WebSocket connection errors or errors sent by the bridge, propagating them to the renderer via IPC.\\n   285\\t-   **IPC Errors:** Renderer-side IPC stubs (`claude-code-context.ts`) handle `invoke` rejections or error events from `on` listeners.\\n   286\\t-   **`ClaudeCodeCliAgentLanguageModelLive`:** Maps all received errors to typed `AiProviderError`s.\\n   287\\t-   **Database Errors:** Bridge service catches PGlite errors and sends them as `db_error` WebSocket messages. Main process relays these.\\n   288\\t\\n   289\\t## 8. Security Considerations\\n   290\\t-   The `Claude Bridge Service` is a local WebSocket server. Access should be restricted to `localhost`.\\n   291\\t-   `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` must be handled securely. It's passed to the bridge service's environment, which then passes it to the Claude CLI environment.\\n   292\\t-   SQL queries in the bridge service must use parameterized statements to prevent SQL injection (as per `docs/fixes/024-database-path-synchronization.md`).\\n   293\\t-   The bridge service, having direct file system and network access, should be as minimal as possible and only expose necessary functionality.\\n   294\\t\\n   295\\t## 9. Database Integration Details\\n   296\\t-   **Shared Path:** Both the `PGLiteService` in Electron's main process (for general app data) and the PGlite client in `claude-bridge-service.js` (for Claude-specific message persistence) are configured to use the same base directory structure derived from `app.getPath(\\\"userData\\\")`, ensuring they can access the same `main_v1` database file. See `docs/fixes/024-database-path-synchronization.md`.\\n   297\\t-   **Schema Management:** The `claude-bridge-service.js` includes `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` statements and `ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS` for basic schema creation and migration of `sessions`, `messages`, and `tool_executions` tables relevant to its interactions.\\n   298\\t\\n   299\\t## 10. Testing Strategies\\n   300\\t-   **Bridge Service (`claude-bridge-service.js`):**\\n   301\\t    -   Unit test WebSocket message handling logic.\\n   302\\t    -   Unit test CLI argument building.\\n   303\\t    -   Integration test PTY interaction with a mock CLI script.\\n   304\\t    -   Integration test database operations with an in-memory PGlite instance.\\n   305\\t-   **Electron Main Process (`main-claude-websocket.ts`):**\\n   306\\t    -   Unit test IPC handlers by mocking `event.sender` and the WebSocket client.\\n   307\\t-   **Renderer (`ClaudeCodeCliAgentLanguageModelLive.ts`):**\\n   308\\t    -   Unit test by mocking `window.electronAPI.claudeCode`.\\n   309\\t-   **E2E (Playwright):**\\n   310\\t    -   Requires the bridge service to be running.\\n   311\\t    -   Test full chat flow, including streaming and persistence, by selecting \\\"Claude Code (CLI)\\\" provider in `AgentChatPane`.\\n   312\\t    -   Requires a valid `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` and installed `@anthropic-ai/claude-code` CLI in the test environment.\\n   313\\t\\n   314\\t## 11. Resilient Session Management (NEW)\\n   315\\t\\n   316\\t### 11.1. Overview\\n   317\\tAs of the latest implementation, the Claude Bridge Service now supports **resilient session management** that allows Claude CLI processes to survive Electron application restarts. This addresses the issue of long-running Claude Code tasks being interrupted by Vite rebuilds or manual Electron restarts during development.\\n   318\\t\\n   319\\t### 11.2. Key Features\\n   320\\t-   **PTY Process Persistence:** PTY processes spawned for Claude CLI are no longer automatically terminated when the WebSocket connection from Electron closes.\\n   321\\t-   **Session Tracking:** Active sessions are tracked using the `sessionId` passed from the renderer through IPC.\\n   322\\t-   **Output Buffering:** When Electron is disconnected, CLI output is buffered in memory for later delivery.\\n   323\\t-   **Automatic Reconnection:** When Electron reconnects with the same `sessionId`, it automatically receives any buffered output and continues streaming new output.\\n   324\\t-   **Explicit Cancellation:** PTY processes are only terminated upon explicit cancel requests or natural completion.\\n   325\\t\\n   326\\t### 11.3. Implementation Details\\n   327\\t\\n   328\\t#### Session State Management\\n   329\\t```javascript\\n   330\\t// Bridge service maintains two key data structures:\\n   331\\tconst activeClaudeSessions = new Map(); \\n   332\\t// Map<sessionId, { pty, requestId, bufferedOutput, claudeSessionId }>\\n   333\\t\\n   334\\tconst activeConnections = new Map(); \\n   335\\t// Map<requestId, ws>\\n   336\\t```\\n   337\\t\\n   338\\t#### Enhanced Message Protocol\\n   339\\tThe WebSocket protocol between Electron main and bridge service now includes:\\n   340\\t-   **Session ID in requests:** `{ id: requestId, args: [...], sessionId: \\\"...\\\" }`\\n   341\\t-   **Cancel messages:** `{ type: 'cancel', requestId: \\\"...\\\" }`\\n   342\\t-   **Session query:** `{ type: 'query_active_sessions', sessionIds: [...] }`\\n   343\\t-   **Health check includes session count:** `{ type: 'health', ..., activeSessions: N }`\\n   344\\t\\n   345\\t#### Reconnection Flow\\n   346\\t1. Electron main process sends request with `sessionId`\\n   347\\t2. Bridge checks if session exists and has active PTY\\n   348\\t3. If yes: Updates requestId mapping, sends buffered output, resumes streaming\\n   349\\t4. If no: Spawns new PTY process and creates session entry\\n   350\\t\\n   351\\t### 11.4. Development Mode Enhancements\\n   352\\tTo prevent Vite from interrupting Claude Code sessions during development:\\n   353\\t-   **Vite configs modified:** All three Vite configs (main, preload, renderer) now set `watch: null` in development mode\\n   354\\t-   **Manual restart required:** Developers must manually restart the dev server to see code changes\\n   355\\t-   **Trade-off:** Stability over convenience during Claude Code usage\\n   356\\t\\n   357\\t## 12. Troubleshooting & Known Issues\\n   358\\t-   **CLI Not Found:** The bridge service includes fallback paths but relies on user installation. Error messages guide the user.\\n   359\\t-   **Authentication Errors:** If `claude auth` hasn't been run or API key is invalid, CLI will fail. Errors are propagated.\\n   360\\t-   **Bridge Service Not Running:** Electron main process checks for bridge availability and reports error if not found. `pnpm bridge` and `pnpm bridge:stop` scripts manage the bridge service.\\n   361\\t-   **Database Path Mismatches:** Previously an issue, resolved by synchronized path calculation.\\n   362\\t-   **Timeout Issues:** Addressed by setting a timeout in `main-claude-websocket.ts` for the utility process, and the bridge service itself could implement timeouts for PTY interactions.\\n   363\\t-   **Session Buffer Overflow:** Currently no limit on buffered output size. In production, consider implementing a circular buffer or size limit.\\n   364\\t-   **Orphaned Sessions:** Sessions are kept indefinitely. Consider implementing TTL or cleanup mechanism for old sessions.\\n   365\\t\\n   366\\t## 13. Future Considerations\\n   367\\t-   **SDK Usage in Bridge:** Evaluate replacing direct `node-pty` interaction in the bridge with Jason Kneen's Claude Code SDK for better abstraction and type safety, if performance allows.\\n   368\\t-   **Dynamic Port for Bridge:** Allow configuration of the WebSocket port for the bridge service.\\n   369\\t-   **Health Check Endpoint:** The bridge service has a basic `health` check; this could be expanded to include session details.\\n   370\\t-   **More Robust Error Codes:** Standardize error codes exchanged between Electron and the bridge.\\n   371\\t-   **Alternative to External Bridge:** Re-evaluate `UtilityProcess` from Electron if its network/subprocess capabilities improve sufficiently for direct CLI management from the main process.\\n   372\\t-   **Claude CLI Resume Feature:** The infrastructure is ready to support `claude --resume <session-id>` when the CLI provides session IDs in its output.\\n   373\\t-   **Session Persistence:** Save session state to disk to survive bridge service restarts.\\n   374\\t-   **Resource Management:** Implement limits on concurrent sessions, buffer sizes, and session lifetimes.\\n   375\\t\\n   376\\tThis system, while complex due to the multi-process architecture, provides a robust and isolated way to integrate the powerful Claude Code CLI into OpenAgents Commander, treating it as just another AI provider within the application's flexible AI backend.\\n   377\\t\"}",
  "status": "executed_success"
}

        "content": "     1\t# Feature Flag System\n     2\t\n     3\t## Overview\n     4\t\n     5\tThe Feature Flag System in Commander allows selective enabling and disabling of features at runtime. This system is built using Effect and integrates seamlessly with our configuration and telemetry services.\n     6\t\n     7\t## Architecture\n     8\t\n     9\t### Core Components\n    10\t\n    11\t```\n    12\t┌─────────────────────┐\n    13\t│   FeatureFlag.ts    │  ← Feature enum definitions\n    14\t└──────────┬──────────┘\n    15\t           │\n    16\t           │\n    17\t┌──────────▼──────────┐\n    18\t│ FeatureFlagService  │  ← Service interface\n    19\t└──────────┬──────────┘\n    20\t           │\n    21\t┌──────────▼──────────┐\n    22\t│FeatureFlagServiceImpl │  ← Effect layer implementation\n    23\t└──────────┬──────────┘\n    24\t           │\n    25\t┌──────────▼──────────┐\n    26\t│  useFeatureFlag     │  ← React hook for components\n    27\t└─────────────────────┘\n    28\t```\n    29\t\n    30\t### Service Dependencies\n    31\t\n    32\t```\n    33\tFeatureFlagService\n    34\t    ├── ConfigurationService (reads enabled features)\n    35\t    └── TelemetryService (tracks initialization)\n    36\t```\n    37\t\n    38\t## Feature Definitions\n    39\t\n    40\tAll features are defined in the `Feature` enum:\n    41\t\n    42\t```typescript\n    43\texport enum Feature {\n    44\t  // AI Providers\n    45\t  CLAUDE_CODE_PROVIDER = \"CLAUDE_CODE_PROVIDER\",\n    46\t  OLLAMA_PROVIDER = \"OLLAMA_PROVIDER\",\n    47\t\n    48\t  // UI Panes\n    49\t  CODER_PANE = \"CODER_PANE\",\n    50\t  HAND_TRACKING = \"HAND_TRACKING\",\n    51\t  WALLET_PANE = \"WALLET_PANE\",\n    52\t  DVM_PROVIDER_PANE = \"DVM_PROVIDER_PANE\",\n    53\t  DVM_JOB_HISTORY_PANE = \"DVM_JOB_HISTORY_PANE\",\n    54\t  PREVIOUS_CHATS_PANE = \"PREVIOUS_CHATS_PANE\",\n    55\t}\n    56\t```\n    57\t\n    58\t## Configuration\n    59\t\n    60\tFeature flags are configured through the `FEATURE_FLAGS_ENABLED_LIST` configuration key:\n    61\t\n    62\t```typescript\n    63\t// In ConfigurationServiceImpl.ts\n    64\tyield* _(configService.set(\n    65\t  \"FEATURE_FLAGS_ENABLED_LIST\",\n    66\t  \"CLAUDE_CODE_PROVIDER,CODER_PANE,HAND_TRACKING\"\n    67\t));\n    68\t```\n    69\t\n    70\tThe value is a comma-separated list of feature names. Features not in this list are considered disabled.\n    71\t\n    72\t## Usage\n    73\t\n    74\t### In React Components\n    75\t\n    76\tUse the `useFeatureFlag` hook:\n    77\t\n    78\t```typescript\n    79\timport { useFeatureFlag } from '@/hooks/useFeatureFlag';\n    80\timport { Feature } from '@/services/featureflags/FeatureFlag';\n    81\t\n    82\tfunction MyComponent() {\n    83\t  const [isWalletEnabled, isLoading, error] = useFeatureFlag(Feature.WALLET_PANE);\n    84\t\n    85\t  if (isLoading) return <LoadingSpinner />;\n    86\t  if (error) return <ErrorMessage />;\n    87\t\n    88\t  return isWalletEnabled ? <WalletUI /> : null;\n    89\t}\n    90\t```\n    91\t\n    92\t### In Effect Services\n    93\t\n    94\tInject `FeatureFlagService` and use it directly:\n    95\t\n    96\t```typescript\n    97\tconst loadProviders = Effect.gen(function* (_) {\n    98\t  const featureFlagService = yield* _(FeatureFlagService);\n    99\t  const isOllamaEnabled = yield* _(featureFlagService.isEnabled(Feature.OLLAMA_PROVIDER));\n   100\t\n   101\t  if (isOllamaEnabled) {\n   102\t    // Load Ollama provider\n   103\t  }\n   104\t});\n   105\t```\n   106\t\n   107\t### Conditional UI Rendering\n   108\t\n   109\tThe Hotbar component demonstrates best practices for feature-flagged UI:\n   110\t\n   111\t```typescript\n   112\tconst [isCoderPaneEnabled] = useFeatureFlag(Feature.CODER_PANE);\n   113\t\n   114\treturn (\n   115\t  <div className=\"hotbar\">\n   116\t    {isCoderPaneEnabled ? (\n   117\t      <HotbarItem slotNumber={1} onClick={onToggleCoderPane}>\n   118\t        <CodeXml />\n   119\t      </HotbarItem>\n   120\t    ) : (\n   121\t      <HotbarItem slotNumber={1} isGhost>\n   122\t        <span className=\"h-5 w-5\" />\n   123\t      </HotbarItem>\n   124\t    )}\n   125\t  </div>\n   126\t);\n   127\t```\n   128\t\n   129\tGhost items maintain consistent slot positions even when features are disabled.\n   130\t\n   131\t### Keyboard Shortcuts\n   132\t\n   133\tKeyboard shortcuts should check feature flags before executing:\n   134\t\n   135\t```typescript\n   136\tconst [isWalletEnabled] = useFeatureFlag(Feature.WALLET_PANE);\n   137\t\n   138\tconst handleKeyDown = (event: KeyboardEvent) => {\n   139\t  if (event.key === '3' && event.ctrlKey) {\n   140\t    if (isWalletEnabled) {\n   141\t      toggleWalletPane();\n   142\t    }\n   143\t  }\n   144\t};\n   145\t```\n   146\t\n   147\t## Implementation Details\n   148\t\n   149\t### Service Implementation\n   150\t\n   151\tThe `FeatureFlagServiceLive` layer:\n   152\t1. Reads the enabled features list from configuration on initialization\n   153\t2. Parses the comma-separated string into a Set of Feature enums\n   154\t3. Provides O(1) lookup for feature status\n   155\t4. Tracks initialization via telemetry\n   156\t5. Handles errors gracefully with proper Effect error types\n   157\t\n   158\t### Error Handling\n   159\t\n   160\tThe system defines a custom error type:\n   161\t\n   162\t```typescript\n   163\texport class FeatureFlagError extends Data.TaggedError(\"FeatureFlagError\")<{\n   164\t  message: string;\n   165\t  cause?: unknown;\n   166\t}> {}\n   167\t```\n   168\t\n   169\t### Initialization Flow\n   170\t\n   171\t1. Service is created when the runtime is built\n   172\t2. On first feature check, it initializes by reading configuration\n   173\t3. Initialization is memoized - subsequent checks use cached data\n   174\t4. Telemetry event is sent with enabled features list\n   175\t\n   176\t## Best Practices\n   177\t\n   178\t### 1. Always Handle Loading States\n   179\t\n   180\t```typescript\n   181\tconst [isEnabled, isLoading, error] = useFeatureFlag(Feature.SOME_FEATURE);\n   182\t\n   183\tif (isLoading) {\n   184\t  // Show loading state or default to disabled\n   185\t  return null;\n   186\t}\n   187\t```\n   188\t\n   189\t### 2. Group Related Features\n   190\t\n   191\tWhen multiple UI elements depend on the same feature:\n   192\t\n   193\t```typescript\n   194\t// Good - single feature check at parent level\n   195\tfunction DVMSection() {\n   196\t  const [isDVMEnabled] = useFeatureFlag(Feature.DVM_PROVIDER_PANE);\n   197\t\n   198\t  if (!isDVMEnabled) return null;\n   199\t\n   200\t  return (\n   201\t    <>\n   202\t      <DVMProviderUI />\n   203\t      <DVMHistoryUI />\n   204\t      <DVMSettingsUI />\n   205\t    </>\n   206\t  );\n   207\t}\n   208\t```\n   209\t\n   210\t### 3. Maintain UI Consistency\n   211\t\n   212\tUse ghost/placeholder elements to maintain layout:\n   213\t\n   214\t```typescript\n   215\t{isFeatureEnabled ? (\n   216\t  <ActiveComponent />\n   217\t) : (\n   218\t  <GhostComponent /> // Maintains spacing/layout\n   219\t)}\n   220\t```\n   221\t\n   222\t### 4. [Main Process] Processing user message content: [{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01Nba6vb6nh1UHwZYC2DQmXx","type":"tool_result","content":"     1\t# Feature Flag System\n     2\t\n     3\t## Overview\n     4\t\n     5\tThe Feature Flag System in Commander allows selective enabling and disabling of features at runtime. This system is built using Effect and integrates seamlessly with our configuration and telemetry services.\n     6\t\n     7\t## Architecture\n     8\t\n     9\t### Core Components\n    10\t\n    11\t```\n    12\t┌─────────────────────┐\n    13\t│   FeatureFlag.ts    │  ← Feature enum definitions\n    14\t└──────────┬──────────┘\n    15\t           │\n    16\t           │\n    17\t┌──────────▼──────────┐\n    18\t│ FeatureFlagService  │  ← Service interface\n    19\t└──────────┬──────────┘\n    20\t           │\n    21\t┌──────────▼──────────┐\n    22\t│FeatureFlagServiceImpl │  ← Effect layer implementation\n    23\t└──────────┬──────────┘\n    24\t           │\n    25\t┌──────────▼──────────┐\n    26\t│  useFeatureFlag     │  ← React hook for components\n    27\t└─────────────────────┘\n    28\t```\n    29\t\n    30\t### Service Dependencies\n    31\t\n    32\t```\n    33\tFeatureFlagService\n    34\t    ├── ConfigurationService (reads enabled features)\n    35\t    └── TelemetryService (tracks initialization)\n    36\t```\n    37\t\n    38\t## Feature Definitions\n    39\t\n    40\tAll features are defined in the `Feature` enum:\n    41\t\n    42\t```typescript\n    43\texport enum Feature {\n    44\t  // AI Providers\n    45\t  CLAUDE_CODE_PROVIDER = \"CLAUDE_CODE_PROVIDER\",\n    46\t  OLLAMA_PROVIDER = \"OLLAMA_PROVIDER\",\n    47\t\n    48\t  // UI Panes\n    49\t  CODER_PANE = \"CODER_PANE\",\n    50\t  HAND_TRACKING = \"HAND_TRACKING\",\n    51\t  WALLET_PANE = \"WALLET_PANE\",\n    52\t  DVM_PROVIDER_PANE = \"DVM_PROVIDER_PANE\",\n    53\t  DVM_JOB_HISTORY_PANE = \"DVM_JOB_HISTORY_PANE\",\n    54\t  PREVIOUS_CHATS_PANE = \"PREVIOUS_CHATS_PANE\",\n    55\t}\n    56\t```\n    57\t\n    58\t## Configuration\n    59\t\n    60\tFeature flags are configured through the `FEATURE_FLAGS_ENABLED_LIST` configuration key:\n    61\t\n    62\t```typescript\n    63\t// In ConfigurationServiceImpl.ts\n    64\tyield* _(configService.set(\n    65\t  \"FEATURE_FLAGS_ENABLED_LIST\",\n    66\t  \"CLAUDE_CODE_PROVIDER,CODER_PANE,HAND_TRACKING\"\n    67\t));\n    68\t```\n    69\t\n    70\tThe value is a comma-separated list of feature names. Features not in this list are considered disabled.\n    71\t\n    72\t## Usage\n    73\t\n    74\t### In React Components\n    75\t\n    76\tUse the `useFeatureFlag` hook:\n    77\t\n    78\t```typescript\n    79\timport { useFeatureFlag } from '@/hooks/useFeatureFlag';\n    80\timport { Feature } from '@/services/featureflags/FeatureFlag';\n    81\t\n    82\tfunction MyComponent() {\n    83\t  const [isWalletEnabled, isLoading, error] = useFeatureFlag(Feature.WALLET_PANE);\n    84\t\n    85\t  if (isLoading) return <LoadingSpinner />;\n    86\t  if (error) return <ErrorMessage />;\n    87\t\n    88\t  return isWalletEnabled ? <WalletUI /> : null;\n    89\t}\n    90\t```\n    91\t\n    92\t### In Effect Services\n    93\t\n    94\tInject `FeatureFlagService` and use it directly:\n    95\t\n    96\t```typescript\n    97\tconst loadProviders = Effect.gen(function* (_) {\n    98\t  const featureFlagService = yield* _(FeatureFlagService);\n    99\t  const isOllamaEnabled = yield* _(featureFlagService.isEnabled(Feature.OLLAMA_PROVIDER));\n   100\t\n   101\t  if (isOllamaEnabled) {\n   102\t    // Load Ollama provider\n   103\t  }\n   104\t});\n   105\t```\n   106\t\n   107\t### Conditional UI Rendering\n   108\t\n   109\tThe Hotbar component demonstrates best practices for feature-flagged UI:\n   110\t\n   111\t```typescript\n   112\tconst [isCoderPaneEnabled] = useFeatureFlag(Feature.CODER_PANE);\n   113\t\n   114\treturn (\n   115\t  <div className=\"hotbar\">\n   116\t    {isCoderPaneEnabled ? (\n   117\t      <HotbarItem slotNumber={1} onClick={onToggleCoderPane}>\n   118\t        <CodeXml />\n   119\t      </HotbarItem>\n   120\t    ) : (\n   121\t      <HotbarItem slotNumber={1} isGhost>\n   122\t        <span className=\"h-5 w-5\" />\n   123\t      </HotbarItem>\n   124\t    )}\n   125\t  </div>\n   126\t);\n   127\t```\n   128\t\n   129\tGhost items maintain consistent slot positions even when features are disabled.\n   130\t\n   131\t### Keyboard Shortcuts\n   132\t\n   133\tKeyboard shortcuts should check feature flags before executing:\n   134\t\n   135\t```typescript\n   136\tconst [isWalletEnabled] = useFeatureFlag(Feature.WALLET_PANE);\n   137\t\n   138\tconst handleKeyDown = (event: KeyboardEvent) => {\n   139\t  if (event.key === '3' && event.ctrlKey) {\n   140\t    if (isWalletEnabled) {\n   141\t      toggleWalletPane();\n   142\t    }\n   143\t  }\n   144\t};\n   145\t```\n   146\t\n   147\t## Implementation Details\n   148\t\n   149\t### Service Implementation\n   150\t\n   151\tThe `FeatureFlagServiceLive` layer:\n   152\t1. Reads the enabled features list from configuration on initialization\n   153\t2. Parses the comma-separated string into a Set of Feature enums\n   154\t3. Provides O(1) lookup for feature status\n   155\t4. Tracks initialization via telemetry\n   156\t5. Handles errors gracefully with proper Effect error types\n   157\t\n   158\t### Error Handling\n   159\t\n   160\tThe system defines a custom error type:\n   161\t\n   162\t```typescript\n   163\texport class FeatureFlagError extends Data.TaggedError(\"FeatureFlagError\")<{\n   164\t  message: string;\n   165\t  cause?: unknown;\n   166\t}> {}\n   167\t```\n   168\t\n   169\t### Initialization Flow\n   170\t\n   171\t1. Service is created when the runtime is built\n   172\t2. On first feature check, it initializes by reading configuration\n   173\t3. Initialization is memoized - subsequent checks use cached data\n   174\t4. Telemetry event is sent with enabled features list\n   175\t\n   176\t## Best Practices\n   177\t\n   178\t### 1. Always Handle Loading States\n   179\t\n   180\t```typescript\n   181\tconst [isEnabled, isLoading, error] = useFeatureFlag(Feature.SOME_FEATURE);\n   182\t\n   183\tif (isLoading) {\n   184\t  // Show loading state or default to disabled\n   185\t  return null;\n   186\t}\n   187\t```\n   188\t\n   189\t### 2. Group Related Features\n   190\t\n   191\tWhen multiple UI elements depend on the same feature:\n   192\t\n   193\t```typescript\n   194\t// Good - single feature check at parent level\n   195\tfunction DVMSection() {\n   196\t  const [isDVMEnabled] = useFeatureFlag(Feature.DVM_PROVIDER_PANE);\n   197\t\n   198\t  if (!isDVMEnabled) return null;\n   199\t\n   200\t  return (\n   201\t    <>\n   202\t      <DVMProviderUI />\n   203\t      <DVMHistoryUI />\n   204\t      <DVMSettingsUI />\n   205\t    </>\n   206\t  );\n   207\t}\n   208\t```\n   209\t\n   210\t### 3. Maintain UI Consistency\n   211\t\n   212\tUse ghost/placeholder elements to maintain layout:\n   213\t\n   214\t```typescript\n   215\t{isFeatureEnabled ? (\n   216\t  <ActiveComponent />\n   217\t) : (\n   218\t  <GhostComponent /> // Maintains spacing/layout\n   219\t)}\n   220\t```\n   221\t\n   222\t### 4. Document Feature Dependencies\n   223\t\n   224\t```typescript\n   225\t// This component requires both WALLET_PANE and DVM_PROVIDER_PANE\n   226\tfunction PaymentFlow() {\n   227\t  const [isWalletEnabled] = useFeatureFlag(Feature.WALLET_PANE);\n   228\t  const [isDVMEnabled] = useFeatureFlag(Feature.DVM_PROVIDER_PANE);\n   229\t\n   230\t  if (!isWalletEnabled || !isDVMEnabled) {\n   231\t    return <FeatureDisabledMessage />;\n   232\t  }\n   233\t  // ...\n   234\t}\n   235\t```\n   236\t\n   237\t## Adding New Features\n   238\t\n   239\t1. **Add to Feature Enum**\n   240\t   ```typescript\n   241\t   export enum Feature {\n   242\t     // ...existing features...\n   243\t     MY_NEW_FEATURE = \"MY_NEW_FEATURE\",\n   244\t   }\n   245\t   ```\n   246\t\n   247\t2. **Update Default Configuration** (if needed)\n   248\t   ```typescript\n   249\t   yield* _(configService.set(\n   250\t     \"FEATURE_FLAGS_ENABLED_LIST\",\n   251\t     \"EXISTING_FEATURES,MY_NEW_FEATURE\"\n   252\t   ));\n   253\t   ```\n   254\t\n   255\t3. **Add Feature Checks**\n   256\t   ```typescript\n   257\t   const [isMyFeatureEnabled] = useFeatureFlag(Feature.MY_NEW_FEATURE);\n   258\t   ```\n   259\t\n   260\t4. **Update Documentation**\n   261\t   - Add to this document\n   262\t   - Update release notes\n   263\t   - Note in PR description\n   264\t\n   265\t## Testing\n   266\t\n   267\t### Unit Testing\n   268\t\n   269\tMock the feature flag service:\n   270\t\n   271\t```typescript\n   272\tconst mockFeatureFlagService: FeatureFlagService = {\n   273\t  isEnabled: (flag: Feature) => {\n   274\t    const enabledFeatures = [Feature.CODER_PANE, Feature.HAND_TRACKING];\n   275\t    return Effect.succeed(enabledFeatures.includes(flag));\n   276\t  },\n   277\t  getEnabledFeatures: () => Effect.succeed([Feature.CODER_PANE, Feature.HAND_TRACKING])\n   278\t};\n   279\t```\n   280\t\n   281\t### Manual Testing\n   282\t\n   283\t1. Modify `FEATURE_FLAGS_ENABLED_LIST` in configuration\n   284\t2. Restart the application\n   285\t3. Verify only enabled features appear in UI\n   286\t4. Verify feature name matches enum exactly (case-sensitive)\n   317\t\n   318\t3. Check for initialization errors in console/telemetry\n   319\t\n   320\t### Performance Considerations\n   321\t\n   322\t- Feature checks are O(1) after initialization\n   323\t- Hook results are not memoized - use `useMemo` if needed\n   324\t- Consider checking features at component mount rather than render\n   325\t\n   326\t## Future Enhancements\n   327\t\n   328\t1. **Dynamic Reloading**: Allow feature flag changes without restart\n   329\t2. **User Overrides**: Let power users enable experimental features\n   330\t3. **A/B Testing**: Use feature flags for gradual rollouts\n   331\t4. **Feature Analytics**: Track feature usage through telemetry\n   332\t5. **Feature Dependencies**: Define relationships between features\n   333\t6. **Environment-Specific Defaults**: Different flags for dev/staging/prod\n   334\t"}]

Provides O(1) lookup for feature status\\n   155\\t4. Tracks initialization via telemetry\\n   156\\t5. Handles errors gracefully with proper Effect error types\\n   157\\t\\n   158\\t### Error Handling\\n   159\\t\\n   160\\tThe system defines a custom error type:\\n   161\\t\\n   162\\t```typescript\\n   163\\texport class FeatureFlagError extends Data.TaggedError(\\\"FeatureFlagError\\\")<{\\n   164\\t  message: string;\\n   165\\t  cause?: unknown;\\n   166\\t}> {}\\n   167\\t```\\n   168\\t\\n   169\\t### Initialization Flow\\n   170\\t\\n   171\\t1. Service is created when the runtime is built\\n   172\\t2. On first feature check, it initializes by reading configuration\\n   173\\t3. Initialization is memoized - subsequent checks use cached data\\n   174\\t4. Telemetry event is sent with enabled features list\\n   175\\t\\n   176\\t## Best Practices\\n   177\\t\\n   178\\t### 1. Always Handle Loading States\\n   179\\t\\n   180\\t```typescript\\n   181\\tconst [isEnabled, isLoading, error] = useFeatureFlag(Feature.SOME_FEATURE);\\n   182\\t\\n   183\\tif (isLoading) {\\n   184\\t  // Show loading state or default to disabled\\n   185\\t  return null;\\n   186\\t}\\n   187\\t```\\n   188\\t\\n   189\\t### 2. Group Related Features\\n   190\\t\\n   191\\tWhen multiple UI elements depend on the same feature:\\n   192\\t\\n   193\\t```typescript\\n   194\\t// Good - single feature check at parent level\\n   195\\tfunction DVMSection() {\\n   196\\t  const [isDVMEnabled] = useFeatureFlag(Feature.DVM_PROVIDER_PANE);\\n   197\\t\\n   198\\t  if (!isDVMEnabled) return null;\\n   199\\t\\n   200\\t  return (\\n   201\\t    <>\\n   202\\t      <DVMProviderUI />\\n   203\\t      <DVMHistoryUI />\\n   204\\t      <DVMSettingsUI />\\n   205\\t    </>\\n   206\\t  );\\n   207\\t}\\n   208\\t```\\n   209\\t\\n   210\\t### 3. Maintain UI Consistency\\n   211\\t\\n   212\\tUse ghost/placeholder elements to maintain layout:\\n   213\\t\\n   214\\t```typescript\\n   215\\t{isFeatureEnabled ? (\\n   216\\t  <ActiveComponent />\\n   217\\t) : (\\n   218\\t  <GhostComponent /> // Maintains spacing/layout\\n   219\\t)}\\n   220\\t```\\n   221\\t\\n   222\\t### 4. Document Feature Dependencies\\n   223\\t\\n   224\\t```typescript\\n   225\\t// This component requires both WALLET_PANE and DVM_PROVIDER_PANE\\n   226\\tfunction PaymentFlow() {\\n   227\\t  const [isWalletEnabled] = useFeatureFlag(Feature.WALLET_PANE);\\n   228\\t  const [isDVMEnabled] = useFeatureFlag(Feature.DVM_PROVIDER_PANE);\\n   229\\t\\n   230\\t  if (!isWalletEnabled || !isDVMEnabled) {\\n   231\\t    return <FeatureDisabledMessage />;\\n   232\\t  }\\n   233\\t  // ...\\n   234\\t}\\n   235\\t```\\n   236\\t\\n   237\\t## Adding New Features\\n   238\\t\\n   239\\t1. **Add to Feature Enum**\\n   240\\t   ```typescript\\n   241\\t   export enum Feature {\\n   242\\t     // ...existing features...\\n   243\\t     MY_NEW_FEATURE = \\\"MY_NEW_FEATURE\\\",\\n   244\\t   }\\n   245\\t   ```\\n   246\\t\\n   247\\t2. **Update Default Configuration** (if needed)\\n   248\\t   ```typescript\\n   249\\t   yield* _(configService.set(\\n   250\\t     \\\"FEATURE_FLAGS_ENABLED_LIST\\\",\\n   251\\t     \\\"EXISTING_FEATURES,MY_NEW_FEATURE\\\"\\n   252\\t   ));\\n   253\\t   ```\\n   254\\t\\n   255\\t3. **Add Feature Checks**\\n   256\\t   ```typescript\\n   257\\t   const [isMyFeatureEnabled] = useFeatureFlag(Feature.MY_NEW_FEATURE);\\n   258\\t   ```\\n   259\\t\\n   260\\t4. **Update Documentation**\\n   261\\t   - 10\\t\\n    11\\t```\\n    12\\t┌─────────────────────┐\\n    13\\t│   FeatureFlag.ts    │  ← Feature enum definitions\\n    14\\t└──────────┬──────────┘\\n    15\\t           │\\n    16\\t       {
        "tool_use_id": "toolu_01CcpbNMecofF537YGzDbFmk",
        "type": "tool_result",
        "content": "     1\t_from OAI deep research_\n     2\t\n     3\t# **Integrating PGlite into an Electron (Vite/Electron Forge) App**\n     4\t\n     5\tPGlite is an embeddable WASM‑based Postgres for JavaScript that supports Node and browser persistence . In an Electron app you can instantiate PGlite either in the **main process** (Node context, persisting to the filesystem) or in the **renderer** (browser context, persisting to IndexedDB). You then use the ElectricSQL sync plugin to replicate Postgres data (via “shapes”) into your PGlite tables . Below are best practices and examples for setting this up, along with an EffectTS-based data layer and notes on Vite/Electron bundling.\n     6\t\n     7\t## **1. Instantiating and Persisting PGlite**\n     8\t\n     9\t**Main process (Node)**: Use PGlite.create() or new PGlite() with a filesystem path. In Electron’s main process (or preload with NodeIntegration), you can do:\n    10\t\n    11\t```\n    12\timport { PGlite } from '@electric-sql/pglite';\n    13\timport path from 'path';\n    14\timport { app } from 'electron';\n    15\t\n    16\tconst dataDir = path.join(app.getPath('userData'), 'pglite-db');\n    17\tconst pg = await PGlite.create(`file://${dataDir}`);\n    18\t// Now `pg` is a PGlite Postgres. Create tables or query as usual.\n    19\t```\n    20\t\n    21\t- **file:// vs unprefixed path**: PGlite accepts either a file:// URI or a plain filesystem path for Node storage . Using file:// can avoid “invalid URL scheme” errors. The example above uses Electron’s app.getPath('userData') so data is stored per-user.\n    22\t\n    23\t- After creating the instance, you can execute SQL to create tables. For example:\n    24\t\n    25\t```\n    26\tawait pg.exec(`\n    27\t  CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS threads (\n    28\t    id SERIAL PRIMARY KEY,\n    29\t    title TEXT NOT NULL,\n    30\t    last_updated TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT now()\n    31\t  );\n    32\t`);\n    33\t```\n    34\t\n    35\tThis data will be stored on disk in dataDir. You can query with await pg.query(\"SELECT \\* FROM threads\").\n    36\t\n    37\t**Renderer process (Browser/IndexedDB)**: If NodeIntegration is disabled in renderer, PGlite falls back to browser storage. For persistence use an idb:// URI:\n    38\t\n    39\t```\n    40\timport { PGlite } from '@electric-sql/pglite';\n    41\t\n    42\tconst pg = await PGlite.create(`idb://my-app-db`);\n    43\t// Or: const pg = new PGlite(\"idb://my-app-db\");\n    44\tawait pg.exec(`\n    45\t  CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS messages (\n    46\t    id SERIAL PRIMARY KEY,\n    47\t    thread_id INT,\n    48\t    content TEXT,\n    49\t    created_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT now()\n    50\t  );\n    51\t`);\n    52\t```\n    53\t\n    54\tThis creates a Postgres in IndexedDB under the key my-app-db . (You can also use PGlite.create() with no prefix for in-memory only.) In either process you can run queries normally, e.g. await pg.query(\"SELECT \\* FROM messages\").\n    55\t\n    56\t> **Tip:** According to the PGlite API, dataDir can use file://, idb://, or memory:// to specify storage . The default new PGlite() with no args creates an in-memory DB .\n    57\t\n    58\t## **2. ElectricSQL Sync Integration**\n    59\t\n    60\tElectricSQL provides a sync plugin for PGlite (alpha) that streams Postgres “shapes” into your local PGlite tables. First install and add the plugin to your PGlite instance:\n    61\t\n    62\t```\n    63\tnpm install @electric-sql/pglite-sync\n    64\t```\n    65\t\n    66\t```\n    67\timport { PGlite } from '@electric-sql/pglite';\n    68\timport { electricSync } from '@electric-sql/pglite-sync';\n    69\t\n    70\tconst pg = await PGlite.create({\n    71\t  extensions: { electric: electricSync() }\n    72\t});\n    73\t// Create local tables (must match your Postgres schema)\n    74\tawait pg.exec(`\n    75\t  CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS threads (\n    76\t    id SERIAL PRIMARY KEY,\n    77\t    title TEXT,\n    78\t    last_updated TIMESTAMPTZ\n    79\t  );\n    80\t  CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS messages (\n    81\t    id SERIAL PRIMARY KEY,\n    82\t    thread_id INT REFERENCES threads(id),\n    83\t    content TEXT,\n    84\t    created_at TIMESTAMPTZ\n    85\t  );\n    86\t`);\n    87\t```\n    88\t\n    89\tHere we enabled the ElectricSQL sync extension. Now you can start syncing. For example, to sync server data into both tables in a single transaction, use syncShapesToTables :\n    90\t\n    91\t```\n    92\tconst sync = await pg.electric.syncShapesToTables({\n    93\t  shapes: {\n    94\t    threads: {\n    95\t      shape: {\n    96\t        url: 'https://myserver/api/v1/shape',\n    97\t        params: { table: 'threads' },\n    98\t      },\n    99\t      table: 'threads',\n   100\t      primaryKey: ['id'],\n   101\t    },\n   102\t    messages: {\n   103\t      shape: {\n   104\t        url: 'https://myserver/api/v1/shape',\n   105\t        params: { table: 'messages' },\n   106\t      },\n   107\t      table: 'messages',\n   108\t      primaryKey: ['id'],\n   109\t    },\n   110\t  },\n   111\t  key: 'chat-sync',  // optional key to persist sync state\n   112\t  onInitialSync: () => console.log('Initial sync done'),\n   113\t});\n   114\t// ...later when no longer needed:\n   115\tsync.unsubscribe();\n   116\t```\n   117\t\n   118\tThis will continuously stream updates for the threads and messages tables from the server Postgres into your local PGlite tables, maintaining transactional consistency . (For a single-table sync, you can use pg.electric.syncShapeToTable({...}) in a similar fashion .) The plugin currently supports one-way (server→client) streaming of shapes; local writes in these tables will not be sent back to the server automatically.\n   119\t\n   120\t> **Local‑only tables:** If you have tables that exist only on the client (e.g. draft messages, local settings, etc.), simply create and use them in PGlite as above. Do _not_ include them in the syncShapesToTables config. They will remain purely local and can be read/written via pg.query or live queries. For example, you could have a table local_chat_settings and manage it manually; it will not be affected by ElectricSQL sync.\n   121\t\n   122\t## **3. EffectTS-Friendly Data Layer**\n   123\t\n   124\tTo integrate PGlite with EffectTS, wrap database operations in an Effect.Service. For example, you might define a PGlite service that creates the DB and exposes a query function:\n   125\t\n   126\t```\n   127\timport * as _PGlite from '@electric-sql/pglite';\n   128\timport { Config, Data, Effect } from 'effect';\n   129\t\n   130\tclass PgliteError extends Data.TaggedError('PgliteError')<{ cause: unknown }> {}\n   131\t\n   132\texport class PGliteService extends Effect.Service<PGliteService>()('PGliteService', {\n   133\t  effect: Effect.gen(function* (_) {\n   134\t    const dataDir = yield* Config.string('DATA_DIR');\n   135\t    // Use file:// for Node or idb:// for browser, based on config\n   136\t    const client = yield* Effect.tryPromise({\n   137\t      try: () => _PGlite.PGlite.create(`file://${dataDir}`),\n   138\t      catch: (error) => new PgliteError({ cause: error }),\n   139\t    });\n   140\t    // Return the raw client for advanced use; see below for query helpers\n   141\t    return { client };\n   142\t  }),\n   143\t})();\n   144\t```\n   145\t\n   146\tYou can then use Effect.tryPromise to perform queries and commands safely. For example, a helper to run an arbitrary query might look like:\n   147\t\n   148\t```\n   149\tconst queryEffect = <T>(sql: string, params?: any[]) =>\n   150\t  Effect.tryPromise<T[]>({\n   151\t    try: () => client.query<T>(sql, params),\n   152\t    catch: (error) => new PgliteError({ cause: error }),\n   153\t  });\n   154\t```\n   155\t\n   156\tand use it inside an Effect program:\n   157\t\n   158\t```\n   159\tEffect.gen(function* (_) {\n   160\t  const { client } = yield* _(PGliteService);\n   161\t  // Example: read all threads\n   162\t  const threads = yield* _(Effect.tryPromise({\n   163\t    try: () => client.query<{id:number,title:string}>('SELECT * FROM threads'),\n   164\t    catch: (e) => new PgliteError({ cause: e })\n   165\t  }));\n   166\t  console.log('Threads:', threads.rows);\n   167\t}).pipe(Effect.provideService(PGliteService, PGliteService.default))\n   168\t```\n   169\t\n   170\tFor **transactions**, PGlite supports an interactive .transaction() API . For example:\n   171\t\n   172\t```\n   173\tconst result = yield* _(Effect.tryPromise({\n   174\t  try: () => client.transaction(async (tx) => {\n   175\t    await tx.exec(\"BEGIN\");\n   176\t    await tx.query(\"INSERT INTO threads(title) VALUES('Test thread')\");\n   177\t    const data = await tx.query(\"SELECT * FROM threads\");\n   178\t    await tx.exec(\"COMMIT\");\n   179\t    return data;\n   180\t  }),\n   181\t  catch: (e) => new PgliteError({ cause: e }),\n   182\t}));\n   183\t```\n   184\t\n   185\tThis will commit if the callback resolves, or rollback if it throws . (You can also omit the explicit BEGIN/COMMIT as transaction() handles them automatically.) Wrapping it in Effect.tryPromise makes it compatible with EffectTS.\n   186\t\n   187\tIn summary, structure your data layer with an EffectTS service (or layer) that creates/owns the PGlite client, and use Effect.tryPromise for all asynchronous DB calls (queries, executes, transactions) to capture errors in Effect. A community example of this pattern is shown here , where a PGlite client is created via PGlite.create(...) inside an Effect, and queries are run with Effect.tryPromise.\n   188\t\n   189\t## **4. Local-Only Tables (Messages and Threads)**\n   190\t\n   191\tFor tables that are only stored locally (not part of any sync shape), treat them like any normal PGlite table. For example, to store chat messages locally you could:\n   192\t\n   193\t```\n   194\tawait pg.exec(`\n   195\t  CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS local_messages (\n   196\t    id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),\n   197\t    thread_id INT,\n   198\t    content TEXT,\n   199\t    created_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT now()\n   200\t  );\n   201\t`);\n   202\t```\n   203\t\n   204\tThen queries are straightforward:\n   205\t\n   206\t```\n   207\t// Insert a local message\n   208\tawait pg.exec(\n   209\t  'INSERT INTO local_messages(thread_id, content) VALUES ($1, $2)',\n   210\t  [threadId, messageText]\n   211\t);\n   212\t// Query messages for a thread\n   213\tconst { rows } = await pg.query<{id:string,content:string}>(\n   214\t  'SELECT * FROM local_messages WHERE thread_id = $1 ORDER BY created_at',\n   215\t  [threadId]\n   216\t);\n   217\t```\n   218\t\n   219\tIn an Effect service you would wrap these in effects. For example:\n   220\t\n   221\t```\n   222\tconst addMessage = (threadId: number, text: string) =>\n   223\t  Effect.tryPromise({\n   224\t    try: () => client.exec(\n   225\t      'INSERT INTO local_messages(thread_id, content) VALUES ($1, $2)',\n   226\t      [threadId, text]\n   227\t    ),\n   228\t    catch: e => new PgliteError({ cause: e })\n   229\t  });\n   230\t```\n   231\t\n   232\tBy keeping these tables out of the ElectricSQL sync configuration, they remain purely local. You can query them or join them with synced tables as needed in your app logic.\n   233\t\n   234\t## **5. Vite + Electron Forge Bundling Issues**\n   235\t\n   236\tPGlite’s WASM and ESM nature requires special bundler settings. In Vite config (main and renderer), **exclude** PGlite from dependency optimization. For example, in vite.config.ts:\n   237\t\n   238\t```\n   239\timport { defineConfig } from 'vite';\n   240\texport default defineConfig({\n   241\t  optimizeDeps: {\n   242\t    exclude: ['@electric-sql/pglite'],\n   243\t  },\n   244\t  build: {\n   245\t    // If needed, mark PGlite as external so Vite does not bundle it\n   246\t    rollupOptions: {\n   247\t      external: ['@electric-sql/pglite']\n   248\t    }\n   249\t  }\n   250\t});\n   251\t```\n   252\t\n   253\tThis ensures Vite does not pre-bundle PGlite’s modules (which can break the WASM loading) . In an Electron Forge project using @electron-forge/plugin-vite, apply these settings to both vite.main.config.ts and vite.renderer.config.ts. Also, if Electron’s NodeIntegration is disabled, the renderer is a pure browser context, so use idb:// as shown above.\n   254\t\n   255\t**Path schemes:** If you encounter errors like ERR_INVALID_URL_SCHEME: The URL must be of scheme file, make sure to prefix your data directory with file:// , or pass an absolute path string. Electron Forge and Vite may convert import.meta.url in unexpected ways, so explicitly constructing a file URI for the database directory (using Node’s path and app.getPath) often avoids this issue.\n   256\t\n   257\t**ESM vs CJS:** Electron 28+ supports ES modules natively, so you can use ESM syntax in your main and preload scripts. If targeting older Electron, ensure your Forge Vite plugin is set up for CJS in the main bundle, or use the nodeIntegration and externals workarounds recommended by Forge docs.\n   258\t\n   259\t**Native modules:** If you add Node native modules (e.g. sqlite3, though PGlite itself is WASM), follow Forge/Vite guidelines to externalize them (see Forge docs on “Native Node modules”). In general, treat @electric-sql/pglite as a special case: exclude it from optimization and let it load its WASM at runtime.\n   260\t\n   261\t## **6. Summary**\n   262\t\n   263\tBy instantiating PGlite with a suitable data directory (file or idb), adding the ElectricSQL sync extension, and wrapping queries in an EffectTS service, you can build a local-first Electron app where Postgres data is stored in-process and kept in sync with a remote database. PGlite’s API supports standard SQL operations (queries, exec, transactions) , and the Electric sync plugin provides syncShapeToTable/syncShapesToTables for partial replication . Local-only tables (e.g. messages, threads) are managed the same way via PGlite but simply aren’t included in the sync shape. Finally, watch out for bundler issues: exclude PGlite from Vite’s optimization and use proper URI schemes, and you’ll have a working PGlite/ElectricSQL stack inside Electron.\n   264\t\n   265\t**Sources:** Official PGlite docs and ElectricSQL guides , plus community examples with Vite/Electron and EffectTS , were used to compile these best practices.\n   266\t"

[Main Process] Processing user message content: [{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01CcpbNMecofF537YGzDbFmk","type":"tool_result","content":"     1\t_from OAI deep research_\n     2\t\n     3\t# **Integrating PGlite into an Electron (Vite/Electron Forge) App**\n     4\t\n     5\tPGlite is an embeddable WASM‑based Postgres for JavaScript that supports Node and browser persistence . In an Electron app you can instantiate PGlite either in the **main process** (Node context, persisting to the filesystem) or in the **renderer** (browser context, persisting to IndexedDB). You then use the ElectricSQL sync plugin to replicate Postgres data (via “shapes”) into your PGlite tables . Below are best practices and examples for setting this up, along with an EffectTS-based data layer and notes on Vite/Electron bundling.\n     6\t\n     7\t## **1. Instantiating and Persisting PGlite**\n     8\t\n     9\t**Main process (Node)**: Use PGlite.create() or new PGlite() with a filesystem path. In Electron’s main process (or preload with NodeIntegration), you can do:\n    10\t\n    11\t```\n    12\timport { PGlite } from '@electric-sql/pglite';\n    13\timport path from 'path';\n    14\timport { app } from 'electron';\n    15\t\n    16\tconst dataDir = path.join(app.getPath('userData'), 'pglite-db');\n    17\tconst pg = await PGlite.create(`file://${dataDir}`);\n    18\t// Now `pg` is a PGlite Postgres. Create tables or query as usual.\n    19\t```\n    20\t\n    21\t- **file:// vs unprefixed path**: PGlite accepts either a file:// URI or a plain filesystem path for Node storage . Using file:// can avoid “invalid URL scheme” errors. The example above uses Electron’s app.getPath('userData') so data is stored per-user.\n    22\t\n    23\t- After creating the instance, you can execute SQL to create tables. For example:\n    24\t\n    25\t```\n    26\tawait pg.exec(`\n    27\t  CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS threads (\n    28\t    id SERIAL PRIMARY KEY,\n    29\t    title TEXT NOT NULL,\n    30\t    last_updated TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT now()\n    31\t  );\n    32\t`);\n    33\t```\n    34\t\n    35\tThis data will be stored on disk in dataDir. You can query with await pg.query(\"SELECT \\* FROM threads\").\n    36\t\n    37\t**Renderer process (Browser/IndexedDB)**: If NodeIntegration is disabled in renderer, PGlite falls back to browser storage. For persistence use an idb:// URI:\n    38\t\n    39\t```\n    40\timport { PGlite } from '@electric-sql/pglite';\n    41\t\n    42\tconst pg = await PGlite.create(`idb://my-app-db`);\n    43\t// Or: const pg = new PGlite(\"idb://my-app-db\");\n    44\tawait pg.exec(`\n    45\t  CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS messages (\n    46\t    id SERIAL PRIMARY KEY,\n    47\t    thread_id INT,\n    48\t    content TEXT,\n    49\t    created_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT now()\n    50\t  );\n    51\t`);\n    52\t```\n    53\t\n    54\tThis creates a Postgres in IndexedDB under the key my-app-db . (You can also use PGlite.create() with no prefix for in-memory only.) In either process you can run queries normally, e.g. await pg.query(\"SELECT \\* FROM messages\").\n    55\t\n    56\t> **Tip:** According to the PGlite API, dataDir can use file://, idb://, or memory:// to specify storage . The default new PGlite() with no args creates an in-memory DB .\n    57\t\n    58\t## **2. ElectricSQL Sync Integration**\n    59\t\n    60\tElectricSQL provides a sync plugin for PGlite (alpha) that streams Postgres “shapes” into your local PGlite tables. First install and add the plugin to your PGlite instance:\n    61\t\n    62\t```\n    63\tnpm install @electric-sql/pglite-sync\n    64\t```\n    65\t\n    66\t```\n    67\timport { PGlite } from '@electric-sql/pglite';\n    68\timport { electricSync } from '@electric-sql/pglite-sync';\n    69\t\n    70\tconst pg = await PGlite.create({\n    71\t  extensions: { electric: electricSync() }\n    72\t});\n    73\t// Create local tables (must match your Postgres schema)\n    74\tawait pg.exec(`\n    75\t  CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS threads (\n    76\t    id SERIAL PRIMARY KEY,\n    77\t    title TEXT,\n    78\t    last_updated TIMESTAMPTZ\n    79\t  );\n    80\t  CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS messages (\n    81\t    id SERIAL PRIMARY KEY,\n    82\t    thread_id INT REFERENCES threads(id),\n    83\t    content TEXT,\n    84\t    created_at TIMESTAMPTZ\n    85\t  );\n    86\t`);\n    87\t```\n    88\t\n    89\tHere we enabled the ElectricSQL sync extension. Now you can start syncing. For example, to sync server data into both tables in a single transaction, use syncShapesToTables :\n    90\t\n    91\t```\n    92\tconst sync = await pg.electric.syncShapesToTables({\n    93\t  shapes: {\n    94\t    threads: {\n    95\t      shape: {\n    96\t        url: 'https://myserver/api/v1/shape',\n    97\t        params: { table: 'threads' },\n    98\t      },\n    99\t      table: 'threads',\n   100\t      primaryKey: ['id'],\n   101\t    },\n   102\t    messages: {\n   103\t      shape: {\n   104\t        url: 'https://myserver/api/v1/shape',\n   105\t        params: { table: 'messages' },\n   106\t      },\n   107\t      table: 'messages',\n   108\t      primaryKey: ['id'],\n   109\t    },\n   110\t  },\n   111\t  key: 'chat-sync',  // optional key to persist sync state\n   112\t  onInitialSync: () => console.log('Initial sync done'),\n   113\t});\n   114\t// ...later when no longer needed:\n   115\tsync.unsubscribe();\n   116\t```\n   117\t\n   118\tThis will continuously stream updates for the threads and messages tables from the server Postgres into your local PGlite tables, maintaining transactional consistency . (For a single-table sync, you can use pg.electric.syncShapeToTable({...}) in a similar fashion .) The plugin currently supports one-way (server→client) streaming of shapes; local writes in these tables will not be sent back to the server automatically.\n   119\t\n   120\t> **Local‑only tables:** If you have tables that exist only on the client (e.g. draft messages, local settings, etc.), simply create and use them in PGlite as above. Do _not_ include them in the syncShapesToTables config. They will remain purely local and can be read/written via pg.query or live queries. For example, you could have a table local_chat_settings and manage it manually; it will not be affected by ElectricSQL sync.\n   121\t\n   122\t## **3. EffectTS-Friendly Data Layer**\n   123\t\n   124\tTo integrate PGlite with EffectTS, wrap database operations in an Effect.Service. For example, you might define a PGlite service that creates the DB and exposes a query function:\n   125\t\n   126\t```\n   127\timport * as _PGlite from '@electric-sql/pglite';\n   128\timport { Config, Data, Effect } from 'effect';\n   129\t\n   130\tclass PgliteError extends Data.TaggedError('PgliteError')<{ cause: unknown }> {}\n   131\t\n   132\texport class PGliteService extends Effect.Service<PGliteService>()('PGliteService', {\n   133\t  effect: Effect.gen(function* (_) {\n   134\t    const dataDir = yield* Config.string('DATA_DIR');\n   135\t    // Use file:// for Node or idb:// for browser, based on config\n   136\t    const client = yield* Effect.tryPromise({\n   137\t      try: () => _PGlite.PGlite.create(`file://${dataDir}`),\n   138\t      catch: (error) => new PgliteError({ cause: error }),\n   139\t    });\n   140\t    // Return the raw client for advanced use; see below for query helpers\n   141\t    return { client };\n   142\t  }),\n   143\t})();\n   144\t```\n   145\t\n   146\tYou can then use Effect.tryPromise to perform queries and commands safely. For example, a helper to run an arbitrary query might look like:\n   147\t\n   148\t```\n   149\tconst queryEffect = <T>(sql: string, params?: any[]) =>\n   150\t  Effect.tryPromise<T[]>({\n   151\t    try: () => client.query<T>(sql, params),\n   152\t    catch: (error) => new PgliteError({ cause: error }),\n   153\t  });\n   154\t```\n   155\t\n   156\tand use it inside an Effect program:\n   157\t\n   158\t```\n   159\tEffect.gen(function* (_) {\n   160\t  const { client } = yield* _(PGliteService);\n   161\t  // Example: read all threads\n   162\t  const threads = yield* _(Effect.tryPromise({\n   163\t    try: () => client.query<{id:number,title:string}>('SELECT * FROM threads'),\n   164\t    catch: (e) => new PgliteError({ cause: e })\n   165\t  }));\n   166\t  console.log('Threads:', threads.rows);\n   167\t}).pipe(Effect.provideService(PGliteService, PGliteService.default))\n   168\t```\n   169\t\n   170\tFor **transactions**, PGlite supports an interactive .transaction() API . For example:\n   171\t\n   172\t```\n   173\tconst result = yield* _(Effect.tryPromise({\n   174\t  try: () => client.transaction(async (tx) => {\n   175\t    await tx.exec(\"BEGIN\");\n   176\t    await tx.query(\"INSERT INTO threads(title) VALUES('Test thread')\");\n   177\t    const data = await tx.query(\"SELECT * FROM threads\");\n   178\t    await tx.exec(\"COMMIT\");\n   179\t    return data;\n   180\t  }),\n   181\t  catch: (e) => new PgliteError({ cause: e }),\n   182\t}));\n   183\t```\n   184\t\n   185\tThis will commit if the callback resolves, or rollback if it throws . (You can also omit the explicit BEGIN/COMMIT as transaction() handles them automatically.) Wrapping it in Effect.tryPromise makes it compatible with EffectTS.\n   186\t\n   187\tIn summary, structure your data layer with an EffectTS service (or layer) that creates/owns the PGlite client, and use Effect.tryPromise for all asynchronous DB calls (queries, executes, transactions) to capture errors in Effect. A community example of this pattern is shown here , where a PGlite client is created via PGlite.create(...) inside an Effect, and queries are run with Effect.tryPromise.\n   188\t\n   189\t## **4. Local-Only Tables (Messages and Threads)**\n   190\t\n   191\tFor tables that are only stored locally (not part of any sync shape), treat them like any normal PGlite table. For example, to store chat messages locally you could:\n   192\t\n   193\t```\n   194\tawait pg.exec(`\n   195\t  CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS local_messages (\n   196\t    id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),\n   197\t    thread_id INT,\n   198\t    content TEXT,\n   199\t    created_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT now()\n   200\t  );\n   201\t`);\n   202\t```\n   203\t\n   204\tThen queries are straightforward:\n   205\t\n   206\t```\n   207\t// Insert a local message\n   208\tawait pg.exec(\n   209\t  'INSERT INTO local_messages(thread_id, content) VALUES ($1, $2)',\n   210\t  [threadId, messageText]\n   211\t);\n   212\t// Query messages for a thread\n   213\tconst { rows } = await pg.query<{id:string,content:string}>(\n   214\t  'SELECT * FROM local_messages WHERE thread_id = $1 ORDER BY created_at',\n   215\t  [threadId]\n   216\t);\n   217\t```\n   218\t\n   219\tIn an Effect service you would wrap these in effects. For example:\n   220\t\n   221\t```\n   222\tconst addMessage = (threadId: number, text: string) =>\n   223\t  Effect.tryPromise({\n   224\t    try: () => client.exec(\n   225\t      'INSERT INTO local_messages(thread_id, content) VALUES ($1, $2)',\n   226\t      [threadId, text]\n   227\t    ),\n   228\t    catch: e => new PgliteError({ cause: e })\n   229\t  });\n   230\t```\n   231\t\n   232\tBy keeping these tables out of the ElectricSQL sync configuration, they remain purely local. You can query them or join them with synced tables as needed in your app logic.\n   233\t\n   234\t## **5. Vite + Electron Forge Bundling Issues**\n   235\t\n   236\tPGlite’s WASM and ESM nature requires special bundler settings. In Vite config (main and renderer), **exclude** PGlite from dependency optimization. For example, in vite.config.ts:\n   237\t\n   238\t```\n   239\timport { defineConfig } from 'vite';\n   240\texport default defineConfig({\n   241\t  optimizeDeps: {\n   242\t    exclude: ['@electric-sql/pglite'],\n   243\t  },\n   244\t  build: {\n   245\t    // If needed, mark PGlite as external so Vite does not bundle it\n   246\t    rollupOptions: {\n   247\t      external: ['@electric-sql/pglite']\n   248\t    }\n   249\t  }\n   250\t});\n   251\t```\n   252\t\n   253\tThis ensures Vite does not pre-bundle PGlite’s modules (which can break the WASM loading) . In an Electron Forge project using @electron-forge/plugin-vite, apply these settings to both vite.main.config.ts and vite.renderer.config.ts. Also, if Electron’s NodeIntegration is disabled, the renderer is a pure browser context, so use idb:// as shown above.\n   254\t\n   255\t**Path schemes:** If you encounter errors like ERR_INVALID_URL_SCHEME: The URL must be of scheme file, make sure to prefix your data directory with file:// , or pass an absolute path string. Electron Forge and Vite may convert import.meta.url in unexpected ways, so explicitly constructing a file URI for the database directory (using Node’s path and app.getPath) often avoids this issue.\n   256\t\n   257\t**ESM vs CJS:** Electron 28+ supports ES modules natively, so you can use ESM syntax in your main and preload scripts. If targeting older Electron, ensure your Forge Vite plugin is set up for CJS in the main bundle, or use the nodeIntegration and externals workarounds recommended by Forge docs.\n   258\t\n   259\t**Native modules:** If you add Node native modules (e.g. sqlite3, though PGlite itself is WASM), follow Forge/Vite guidelines to externalize them (see Forge docs on “Native Node modules”). In general, treat @electric-sql/pglite as a special case: exclude it from optimization and let it load its WASM at runtime.\n   260\t\n   261\t## **6. Summary**\n   262\t\n   263\tBy instantiating PGlite with a suitable data directory (file or idb), adding the ElectricSQL sync extension, and wrapping queries in an EffectTS service, you can build a local-first Electron app where Postgres data is stored in-process and kept in sync with a remote database. PGlite’s API supports standard SQL operations (queries, exec, transactions) , and the Electric sync plugin provides syncShapeToTable/syncShapesToTables for partial replication . Local-only tables (e.g. messages, threads) are managed the same way via PGlite but simply aren’t included in the sync shape. Finally, watch out for bundler issues: exclude PGlite from Vite’s optimization and use proper URI schemes, and you’ll have a working PGlite/ElectricSQL stack inside Electron.\n   264\t\n   265\t**Sources:** Official PGlite docs and ElectricSQL guides , plus community examples with Vite/Electron and EffectTS , were used to compile these best practices.\n   266\t"}]

For example:\\n   171\\t\\n   172\\t```\\n   173\\tconst result = yield* _(Effect.tryPromise({\\n   174\\t  try: () => client.transaction(async (tx) => {\\n   175\\t    await tx.exec(\\\"BEGIN\\\");\\n   176\\t    await tx.query(\\\"INSERT INTO threads(title) VALUES('Test thread')\\\");\\n   177\\t    const data = await tx.query(\\\"SELECT * FROM threads\\\");\\n   178\\t    await tx.exec(\\\"COMMIT\\\");\\n   179\\t    return data;\\n   180\\t  }),\\n   181\\t  catch: (e) => new PgliteError({ cause: e }),\\n   182\\t}));\\n   183\\t```\\n   184\\t\\n   185\\tThis will commit if the callback resolves, or rollback if it throws . (You can also omit the explicit BEGIN/COMMIT as transaction() handles them automatically.) Wrapping it in Effect.tryPromise makes it compatible with EffectTS.\\n   186\\t\\n   187\\tIn summary, structure your data layer with an EffectTS service (or layer) that creates/owns the PGlite client, and use Effect.tryPromise for all asynchronous DB calls (queries, executes, transactions) to capture errors in Effect. A community example of this pattern is shown here , where a PGlite client is created via PGlite.create(...) inside an Effect, and queries are run with Effect.tryPromise.\\n   188\\t\\n   189\\t## **4. Local-Only Tables (Messages and Threads)**\\n   190\\t\\n   191\\tFor tables that are only stored locally (not part of any sync shape), treat them like any normal PGlite table. For example, to store chat messages locally you could:\\n   192\\t\\n   193\\t```\\n   194\\tawait pg.exec(`\\n   195\\t  CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS local_messages (\\n   196\\t    id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),\\n   197\\t    thread_id INT,\\n   198\\t    content TEXT,\\n   199\\t    created_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT now()\\n   200\\t  );\\n   201\\t`);\\n   202\\t```\\n   203\\t\\n   204\\tThen queries are straightforward:\\n   205\\t\\n   206\\t```\\n   207\\t// Insert a local message\\n   208\\tawait pg.exec(\\n   209\\t  'INSERT INTO local_messages(thread_id, content) VALUES ($1, $2)',\\n   210\\t  [threadId, messageText]\\n   211\\t);\\n   212\\t// Query messages for a thread\\n   213\\tconst { rows } = await pg.query<{id:string,content:string}>(\\n   214\\t  'SELECT * FROM local_messages WHERE thread_id = $1 ORDER BY created_at',\\n   215\\t  [threadId]\\n   216\\t);\\n   217\\t```\\n   218\\t\\n   219\\tIn an Effect service you would wrap these in effects. For example:\\n   220\\t\\n   221\\t```\\n   222\\tconst addMessage = (threadId: number, text: string) =>\\n   223\\t  Effect.tryPromise({\\n   224\\t    try: () => client.exec(\\n   225\\t      'INSERT INTO local_messages(thread_id, content) VALUES ($1, $2)',\\n   226\\t      [threadId, text]\\n   227\\t    ),\\n   228\\t    catch: e => new PgliteError({ cause: e })\\n   229\\t  });\\n   230\\t```\\n   231\\t\\n   232\\tBy keeping these tables out of the ElectricSQL sync configuration, they remain purely local. You can query them or join them with synced tables as needed in your app logic.\\n   233\\t\\n   234\\t## **5. Vite + Electron Forge Bundling Issues**\\n   235\\t\\n   236\\tPGlite’s WASM and ESM nature requires special bundler settings. In Vite config (main and renderer), **exclude** PGlite from dependency optimization. For example, in vite.config.ts:\\n   237\\t\\n   238\\t```\\n   239\\timport { defineConfig } from 'vite';\\n   240\\texport default defineConfig({\\n   241\\t  optimizeDeps: {\\n   242\\t    exclude: ['@electric-sql/pglite'],\\n   243\\t  },\\n   244\\t  build: {\\n   245\\t    // If needed, mark PGlite as external so Vite does not bundle it\\n   246\\t    rollupOptions: {\\n   247\\t      external: ['@electric-sql/pglite']\\n   248\\t    }\\n   249\\t  }\\n   250\\t});\\n   251\\t```\\n   252\\t\\n   253\\tThis ensures Vite does not pre-bundle PGlite’s modules (which can break the WASM loading) . In an Electron Forge project using @electron-forge/plugin-vite, apply these settings to both vite.main.config.ts and vite.renderer.config.ts. Also, if Electron’s NodeIntegration is disabled, the renderer is a pure browser context, so use idb:// as shown above.\\n   254\\t\\n   255\\t**Path schemes:** If you encounter errors like ERR_INVALID_URL_SCHEME: The URL must be of scheme file, make sure to prefix your data directory with file:// , or pass an absolute path string. Electron Forge and Vite may convert import.meta.url in unexpected ways, so explicitly constructing a file URI for the database directory (using Node’s path and app.getPath) often avoids this issue.\\n   256\\t\\n   257\\t**ESM vs CJS:** Electron 28+ supports ES modules natively, so you can use ESM syntax in your main and preload scripts. If targeting older Electron, ensure your Forge Vite plugin is set up for CJS in the main bundle, or use the nodeIntegration and externals workarounds recommended by Forge docs.\\n   258\\t\\n   259\\t**Native modules:** If you add Node native modules (e.g. sqlite3, though PGlite itself is WASM), follow Forge/Vite guidelines to externalize them (see Forge docs on “Native Node modules”). In general, treat @electric-sql/pglite as a special case: exclude it from optimization and let it load its WASM at runtime.\\n   260\\t\\n   261\\t## **6. Summary**\\n   262\\t\\n   263\\tBy instantiating PGlite with a suitable data directory (file or idb), adding the ElectricSQL sync extension, and wrapping queries in an EffectTS service, you can build a local-first Electron app where Postgres data is stored in-process and kept in sync with a remote database. PGlite’s API supports standard SQL operations (queries, exec, transactions) , and the Electric sync plugin provides syncShapeToTable/syncShapesToTables for partial replication . Local-only tables (e.g. messages, threads) are managed the same way via PGlite but simply aren’t included in the sync shape. Finally, watch out for bundler issues: exclude PGlite from Vite’s optimization and use proper URI schemes, and you’ll have a working PGlite/ElectricSQL stack inside Electron.\\n   264\\t\\n   265\\t**Sources:** Official PGlite docs and ElectricSQL guides , plus community examples with Vite/Electron and EffectTS , were used to compile these best practices.\\n   266\\t\"}","status":"executed_success"}}
